<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: Niemann-Pick Disease Type A/B
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of lysosomal storage diseases, specifically Niemann-Pick disease types A and B, caused by mutations in the *SMPD1* gene leading to deficient acid sphingomyelinase (ASM) activity, resulting in the accumulation of sphingomyelin within lysosomes, particularly in macrophages (foam cells), leading to hepatosplenomegaly and neurological symptoms (Type A). (本题考察溶酶体贮积症，特别是A型和B型尼曼-匹克病，由*SMPD1*基因突变引起，导致酸性神经鞘磷脂酶(ASM)活性不足，从而导致溶酶体中神经鞘磷脂的积累，尤其是在巨噬细胞（泡沫细胞）中，导致肝脾肿大和神经系统症状（A型）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-year-old boy presents to the pediatric clinic with progressive developmental delay, hypotonia, and feeding difficulties. His parents report that he has always been a "floppy baby" and has difficulty sitting up unsupported. Physical examination reveals significant hepatosplenomegaly, and a cherry-red spot is noted on fundoscopic examination. Laboratory tests show elevated serum cholesterol and triglycerides. A skin biopsy is performed, and electron microscopy reveals characteristic foamy macrophages packed with lamellar inclusions within the lysosomes of fibroblasts. Enzyme activity assays on leukocytes show markedly reduced acid sphingomyelinase (ASM) activity (less than 5% of normal). Genetic testing reveals compound heterozygous mutations in the *SMPD1* gene.

Which of the following biochemical pathways is most directly disrupted in this patient, leading to the accumulation of the observed intracellular inclusions?

(A) Glycogenolysis, due to deficiency of acid maltase (α-glucosidase), leading to glycogen accumulation in lysosomes.
(B) Ganglioside degradation, due to deficiency of β-hexosaminidase A, leading to GM2 ganglioside accumulation in lysosomes.
(C) Sphingolipid metabolism, due to deficiency of acid sphingomyelinase, leading to sphingomyelin accumulation in lysosomes.
(D) Cholesterol synthesis, due to deficiency of HMG-CoA reductase, leading to impaired cholesterol production.
(E) Purine degradation, due to deficiency of hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase (HGPRT), leading to uric acid accumulation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 2-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, feeding difficulties, hepatosplenomegaly, cherry-red spot on fundoscopy. These symptoms point towards a lysosomal storage disease affecting the central nervous system and visceral organs.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated serum cholesterol and triglycerides are common in lysosomal storage diseases affecting lipid metabolism.
- **Skin Biopsy & Electron Microscopy:** Foamy macrophages with lamellar inclusions in lysosomes are pathognomonic for Niemann-Pick disease, indicating the accumulation of a lipid substrate within lysosomes.
- **Enzyme Activity Assay:** Markedly reduced acid sphingomyelinase (ASM) activity confirms the specific enzyme deficiency.
- **Genetic Testing:** Compound heterozygous mutations in the *SMPD1* gene, which encodes ASM, confirm the diagnosis of Niemann-Pick disease type A or B.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical pathway disrupted by the enzyme deficiency leading to the intracellular inclusions.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Niemann-Pick disease types A and B are autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorders caused by mutations in the *SMPD1* gene.
- The *SMPD1* gene encodes the lysosomal enzyme acid sphingomyelinase (ASM).
- ASM is responsible for hydrolyzing sphingomyelin into ceramide and phosphorylcholine within the lysosome. Sphingomyelin is a major phospholipid component of cell membranes, particularly myelin sheaths.
- Deficiency of ASM leads to the accumulation of sphingomyelin within the lysosomes of various cells, especially macrophages.
- These lipid-laden macrophages appear "foamy" under microscopy and are characteristic of the disease.
- The accumulation of sphingomyelin causes lysosomal enlargement and dysfunction, leading to cellular damage and organomegaly (hepatosplenomegaly).
- In Niemann-Pick disease type A (the more severe form, often presenting in infancy), sphingomyelin accumulates in the brain, leading to severe neurodegeneration, developmental regression, hypotonia, and early death. The cherry-red spot is also characteristic.
- In Niemann-Pick disease type B (a milder form), the primary manifestations are visceral, with hepatosplenomegaly and pulmonary involvement, but typically without significant neurological involvement.
- The patient's presentation (developmental delay, hypotonia, hepatosplenomegaly, cherry-red spot, foamy macrophages, low ASM activity, *SMPD1* mutations) is classic for Niemann-Pick disease type A.
- The underlying biochemical defect is the impaired hydrolysis of sphingomyelin due to deficient ASM, leading to its accumulation within lysosomes. Therefore, the disrupted pathway is sphingolipid metabolism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Glycogenolysis is the breakdown of glycogen. Deficiency of acid maltase (α-glucosidase) causes Pompe disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type II), characterized by glycogen accumulation in lysosomes, leading to muscle weakness (cardiomyopathy, hypotonia). While hypotonia is present, the other features (hepatosplenomegaly, cherry-red spot, foamy macrophages, sphingomyelin accumulation) are inconsistent with Pompe disease.
- (B): Ganglioside degradation involves enzymes like β-hexosaminidase A. Deficiency of β-hexosaminidase A causes Tay-Sachs disease, characterized by the accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in lysosomes, primarily affecting neurons. Symptoms include developmental regression, hypotonia, seizures, and a cherry-red spot. However, Tay-Sachs typically does not cause significant hepatosplenomegaly or foamy macrophages containing sphingomyelin. The specific enzyme deficiency (ASM) and accumulated substrate (sphingomyelin) are different.
- (D): Cholesterol synthesis is the process of creating cholesterol. Deficiency of HMG-CoA reductase is the target of statin drugs and can cause impaired cholesterol production, but it is not a lysosomal storage disease. It does not lead to the accumulation of sphingomyelin or foamy macrophages.
- (E): Purine degradation is the breakdown of purines. Deficiency of hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase (HGPRT) causes Lesch-Nyhan syndrome, an X-linked recessive disorder characterized by hyperuricemia, gout, neurological dysfunction (self-mutilation, choreoathetosis), and developmental delay. It involves purine metabolism, not sphingolipid metabolism, and does not cause sphingomyelin accumulation or foamy macrophages.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Niemann-Pick disease types A and B are caused by acid sphingomyelinase (ASM) deficiency, leading to sphingomyelin accumulation in lysosomes. Key features include hepatosplenomegaly, foamy macrophages, and neurological symptoms (especially in Type A). Differentiate from other lysosomal storage diseases like Tay-Sachs (GM2 ganglioside, β-hexosaminidase A deficiency) and Gaucher disease (glucocerebroside, glucocerebrosidase deficiency), which also cause hepatosplenomegaly but have different accumulated substrates and cellular findings. Pompe disease involves glycogen accumulation.

<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: Gaucher Disease Type 1
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Lysosomal storage diseases, specifically Gaucher disease Type 1, caused by deficiency of glucocerebrosidase (GBA), leading to accumulation of glucocerebroside in macrophages (Gaucher cells), resulting in hepatosplenomegaly, cytopenias, and bone disease. (溶酶体贮积病，特别是高雪氏病1型，由葡糖脑苷脂酶（GBA）缺乏引起，导致葡糖脑苷脂在巨噬细胞中积累（高雪氏细胞），从而引起肝脾肿大、细胞减少症和骨骼疾病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old Ashkenazi Jewish man presents to his primary care physician complaining of chronic fatigue, easy bruising, and recurrent nosebleeds over the past year. He denies fever, weight loss, or night sweats. His past medical history is unremarkable except for mild anemia diagnosed in childhood. Family history is significant for his maternal aunt who died at age 50 from "bone problems." Physical examination reveals significant splenomegaly (palpable 10 cm below the left costal margin) and hepatomegaly (palpable 5 cm below the right costal margin). Neurological examination is normal. Complete blood count (CBC) shows hemoglobin 10.5 g/dL (normal 13.5-17.5 g/dL), platelet count 80,000/µL (normal 150,000-450,000/µL), and white blood cell count 4,000/µL (normal 4,500-11,000/µL). Peripheral blood smear shows "sea-blue" macrophages. A bone marrow biopsy reveals the presence of large, lipid-laden macrophages with characteristic "wrinkled tissue paper" cytoplasm. Enzyme activity assay of leukocytes shows markedly reduced glucocerebrosidase activity (<5% of normal). Genetic testing reveals compound heterozygosity for two different pathogenic mutations in the *GBA* gene. Which of the following is the most likely underlying molecular defect causing this patient's condition?

(A) Deficiency in sphingomyelinase, leading to accumulation of sphingomyelin in lysosomes.
(B) Deficiency in hexosaminidase A, leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in neurons.
(C) Deficiency in glucocerebrosidase, leading to accumulation of glucocerebroside in macrophages.
(D) Deficiency in α-galactosidase A, leading to accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) in endothelial cells.
(E) Deficiency in β-hexosaminidase A and B, leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in various tissues.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Demographics & History:** 35-year-old Ashkenazi Jewish man. Ashkenazi Jewish ancestry is a high-risk factor for Gaucher disease. Chronic fatigue, easy bruising, recurrent nosebleeds suggest cytopenias (anemia, thrombocytopenia). Mild anemia in childhood is also suggestive. Family history of "bone problems" in a maternal aunt is consistent with skeletal manifestations of Gaucher disease.
- **Physical Examination:** Significant splenomegaly and hepatomegaly are classic signs of Gaucher disease due to infiltration by Gaucher cells. Normal neurological examination points towards Type 1 Gaucher disease (non-neuronopathic).
- **Laboratory Findings:** CBC confirms pancytopenia (anemia, thrombocytopenia, leukopenia). Peripheral smear showing "sea-blue" macrophages (macrophages containing partially degraded lipids) is characteristic. Bone marrow biopsy showing large, lipid-laden macrophages with "wrinkled tissue paper" cytoplasm confirms the presence of Gaucher cells.
- **Enzyme Assay:** Markedly reduced glucocerebrosidase activity is the definitive biochemical finding for Gaucher disease.
- **Genetic Testing:** Compound heterozygosity in the *GBA* gene confirms the genetic basis of the enzyme deficiency.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying molecular defect.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The patient's clinical presentation (splenomegaly, hepatomegaly, pancytopenia, bone involvement, Ashkenazi Jewish ancestry), characteristic bone marrow findings (Gaucher cells), and markedly reduced glucocerebrosidase activity strongly indicate Gaucher disease Type 1.
- Gaucher disease is a lysosomal storage disorder caused by mutations in the *GBA* gene, which encodes the enzyme glucocerebrosidase (also known as β-glucosidase).
- Glucocerebrosidase is located in the lysosomes and is responsible for hydrolyzing glucocerebroside (a glycosphingolipid) into glucose and ceramide.
- Deficiency of glucocerebrosidase leads to the accumulation of glucocerebroside within the lysosomes of macrophages, primarily in the spleen, liver, bone marrow, and lymph nodes.
- These lipid-laden macrophages are called Gaucher cells and are characterized by their large size and "wrinkled tissue paper" appearance on microscopy due to the accumulation of glucocerebroside.
- The accumulation of Gaucher cells causes infiltration and enlargement of affected organs (hepatosplenomegaly), impairs hematopoiesis (pancytopenia), and leads to bone lesions (bone pain, fractures, avascular necrosis).
- Type 1 Gaucher disease is the most common form and is characterized by the absence of primary neurological involvement. It is typically inherited in an autosomal recessive manner.
- The specific molecular defect is the deficiency of the glucocerebrosidase enzyme due to mutations in the *GBA* gene, leading to the accumulation of its substrate, glucocerebroside.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency in sphingomyelinase causes Niemann-Pick disease (Types A and B). This leads to the accumulation of sphingomyelin in lysosomes, primarily affecting the liver, spleen, lungs, and brain (Type A). Symptoms include hepatosplenomegaly, neurological deterioration (Type A), and sometimes pulmonary involvement. The characteristic cells are foam cells, not Gaucher cells. The enzyme assay would show deficient sphingomyelinase activity.
- (B) Deficiency in hexosaminidase A causes Tay-Sachs disease. This leads to the accumulation of GM2 ganglioside primarily in neurons, causing progressive neurodegeneration, cherry-red spot on the macula, and early death. The enzyme assay would show deficient hexosaminidase A activity. The clinical presentation (neurological symptoms, age of onset) and affected cells (neurons) are different.
- (D) Deficiency in α-galactosidase A causes Fabry disease. This leads to the accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) in endothelial cells, smooth muscle cells, and neurons. Symptoms include angiokeratomas, acroparesthesias, hypohidrosis, corneal opacities, renal failure, and cardiovascular disease. The enzyme assay would show deficient α-galactosidase A activity. The clinical presentation and accumulated substrate are different.
- (E) Deficiency in β-hexosaminidase A and B causes Sandhoff disease. This is similar to Tay-Sachs disease but also involves accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in visceral organs due to the deficiency of both hexosaminidase A and B. It presents with severe neurodegeneration and visceral involvement. The enzyme assay would show deficient hexosaminidase A and B activity. The clinical presentation (neurological symptoms, age of onset) and affected cells (neurons, visceral organs) are different.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Gaucher disease Type 1 is caused by deficient glucocerebrosidase activity, leading to glucocerebroside accumulation in macrophages (Gaucher cells). Key features include hepatosplenomegaly, pancytopenia, bone disease, and absence of primary neurological involvement. It is important to differentiate it from other lysosomal storage diseases like Niemann-Pick disease (sphingomyelinase deficiency, sphingomyelin accumulation), Tay-Sachs disease (hexosaminidase A deficiency, GM2 ganglioside accumulation in neurons), and Fabry disease (α-galactosidase A deficiency, Gb3 accumulation). Remember the specific enzyme deficiency, accumulated substrate, affected cell type, and clinical manifestations for each disorder.

---

<a id='question-139'></a>

### Question 139: Fabry Disease: X-Linked Recessive GLA Mutation, Alpha-Galactosidase A Deficiency, Globotriaosylceramide (Gb3), Angiokeratomas, Renal Failure - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular basis, biochemical consequences, and clinical manifestations of Fabry disease, specifically focusing on the accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) due to alpha-galactosidase A deficiency, its inheritance pattern, and associated clinical features. (考察法布里病的分子基础、生化后果和临床表现，特别是由于α-半乳糖苷酶A缺乏导致三酰基葡糖胺（Gb3）积累，其遗传模式和相关临床特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of burning pain in his hands and feet, which worsens with exercise or heat exposure. He also reports episodes of abdominal pain and diarrhea since childhood. Physical examination reveals multiple small, dark red to purple papules (angiokeratomas) on his lower abdomen and scrotum. His renal function tests show elevated serum creatinine (2.5 mg/dL; normal range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL) and proteinuria. His brother has similar symptoms and renal failure requiring dialysis at age 40. His sister is asymptomatic. Enzyme assay reveals significantly reduced alpha-galactosidase A activity in leukocytes. Genetic testing confirms a missense mutation in the *GLA* gene on the X chromosome. Which of the following biochemical mechanisms best explains the accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) in Fabry disease?

(A) Defective lysosomal transport of Gb3 due to mutations in the *NPC1* or *NPC2* gene, leading to impaired trafficking to the lysosome.
(B) Impaired synthesis of ceramide due to deficiency of serine palmitoyltransferase (SPT), a key enzyme in the de novo synthesis pathway.
(C) Defective degradation of Gb3 within lysosomes due to the deficiency of alpha-galactosidase A, the enzyme responsible for hydrolyzing the terminal alpha-galactose residue.
(D) Increased synthesis of Gb3 due to enhanced activity of glucosylceramide synthase, leading to an overload of the lysosomal degradation pathway.
(E) Impaired trafficking of alpha-galactosidase A from the endoplasmic reticulum to the lysosome due to mutations in the *GLA* gene, preventing its proper localization.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old male with burning pain (acroparesthesia), abdominal pain, angiokeratomas, and renal failure. These are classic symptoms of Fabry disease.
*   **Family History:** Affected brother with renal failure, asymptomatic sister. This pattern is consistent with X-linked recessive inheritance, where males are typically affected, and females are usually carriers (though some may show symptoms).
*   **Lab Findings:** Elevated creatinine and proteinuria indicate renal involvement. Reduced alpha-galactosidase A activity confirms the enzyme deficiency characteristic of Fabry disease.
*   **Genetic Testing:** *GLA* gene mutation confirms the diagnosis. The *GLA* gene encodes alpha-galactosidase A.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical mechanism explaining Gb3 accumulation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Fabry disease is an X-linked recessive lysosomal storage disorder caused by mutations in the *GLA* gene, which encodes the enzyme alpha-galactosidase A. This enzyme is located within lysosomes and is responsible for hydrolyzing the terminal alpha-galactose residue from globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) and related glycosphingolipids. Gb3 is synthesized primarily in endothelial cells and pericytes. When alpha-galactosidase A is deficient or non-functional, Gb3 cannot be broken down. Consequently, Gb3 accumulates within lysosomes of various cell types, particularly endothelial cells, smooth muscle cells, podocytes (in the kidney), neurons, and cardiomyocytes. This accumulation leads to cellular dysfunction and damage, manifesting clinically as angiokeratomas (due to accumulation in dermal blood vessels), acroparesthesia (nerve damage), renal failure (podocyte damage), cardiac hypertrophy (cardiomyocyte damage), and gastrointestinal issues. The question asks for the mechanism of Gb3 accumulation. The deficiency of alpha-galactosidase A directly impairs the breakdown of Gb3 within the lysosome. Therefore, the correct mechanism is defective degradation of Gb3 due to the enzyme deficiency.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Defective lysosomal transport of Gb3 due to mutations in the *NPC1* or *NPC2* gene describes Niemann-Pick disease type C. This involves impaired cholesterol trafficking within the cell, leading to accumulation of cholesterol and other lipids, including glycosphingolipids, but the primary defect is not in alpha-galactosidase A activity or Gb3 degradation itself. Fabry disease involves a specific enzyme deficiency leading to Gb3 accumulation, not a general transport defect.
- (B): Impaired synthesis of ceramide due to deficiency of serine palmitoyltransferase (SPT) causes hereditary spastic paraplegia type 7 (HSP7) and potentially other neurological disorders. SPT is involved in the *de novo* synthesis of sphingolipids, including ceramide, which is a precursor for more complex sphingolipids like Gb3. However, Fabry disease is a *degradation* defect, not a synthesis defect. The synthesis pathway is intact; the problem lies in breaking down the end product (Gb3).
- (D): Increased synthesis of Gb3 due to enhanced activity of glucosylceramide synthase would lead to an overload of the lysosomal degradation pathway. While increased synthesis could contribute to substrate accumulation, the primary and defining defect in Fabry disease is the *lack* of functional alpha-galactosidase A, which prevents the breakdown of Gb3, regardless of its synthesis rate. There is no evidence that glucosylceramide synthase activity is increased in Fabry disease.
- (E): Impaired trafficking of alpha-galactosidase A from the endoplasmic reticulum to the lysosome due to mutations in the *GLA* gene. While mutations in the *GLA* gene cause Fabry disease, the primary defect is typically the *activity* or *stability* of the enzyme within the lysosome, not its trafficking *to* the lysosome. Some mutations might affect trafficking, but the most common mechanism involves a non-functional or unstable enzyme within the lysosome, leading to impaired Gb3 degradation. Option (C) directly addresses the core biochemical defect – the inability to break down Gb3.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Fabry disease is caused by a deficiency in lysosomal alpha-galactosidase A, leading to the accumulation of its substrate, globotriaosylceramide (Gb3). This is a classic example of a lysosomal storage disorder. Key clinical features include angiokeratomas, acroparesthesia, renal failure, and cardiac involvement. It is inherited in an X-linked recessive manner. Differential diagnoses include other lysosomal storage disorders (e.g., Gaucher disease, Niemann-Pick disease) and other causes of renal failure or neuropathy. Remember the specific enzyme deficiency (alpha-galactosidase A) and the accumulated substrate (Gb3).

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Integrated Clinical Case: Profound Fasting Hypoglycemia with Undetectable Ketones in a 14-Month-Old Infant with Gastroenteritis - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of fatty acid oxidation disorders, specifically medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (MCAD) deficiency, and its presentation during periods of fasting or illness, leading to hypoketotic hypoglycemia. It requires integrating clinical presentation, biochemical findings (glucose, ketones, acylcarnitine profile), and understanding the metabolic pathway involved. (考察脂肪酸氧化障碍，特别是MCAD缺乏症，以及其在禁食或疾病期间的表现，导致低酮症性低血糖。需要整合临床表现、生化发现（葡萄糖、酮体、酰基肉碱谱）和理解相关的代谢途径。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 14-month-old previously healthy boy presents to the emergency department with lethargy, vomiting, and poor feeding for the past 12 hours. His mother reports he had a mild upper respiratory infection and diarrhea starting 2 days ago. He has not been eating well since then. Vital signs show a temperature of 37.8°C, heart rate of 140 bpm, respiratory rate of 30 breaths/min, and blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg. Physical examination reveals a drowsy but arousable infant with dry mucous membranes and slightly decreased skin turgor. Initial laboratory results show a serum glucose of 35 mg/dL (normal range: 70-100 mg/dL), serum ketones are undetectable (urine ketones are also negative), elevated liver enzymes (AST 150 U/L, ALT 120 U/L; normal ranges: <40 U/L), hyperammonemia (ammonia 150 µmol/L; normal range: 15-50 µmol/L), and mild metabolic acidosis (pH 7.28, bicarbonate 15 mEq/L; normal range: 22-26 mEq/L). A plasma acylcarnitine profile reveals elevated levels of medium-chain acylcarnitines, specifically C6 (hexanoylcarnitine), C8 (octanoylcarnitine), and C10 (decanoylcarnitine). Genetic testing confirms a homozygous missense mutation in the *ACADM* gene. What is the underlying biochemical defect causing this infant's presentation?

(A) Deficiency of carnitine palmitoyltransferase I (CPT I), leading to impaired transport of long-chain fatty acids into the mitochondria.
(B) Deficiency of acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (ACAD), specifically medium-chain ACAD (MCAD), leading to impaired β-oxidation of medium-chain fatty acids.
(C) Deficiency of long-chain 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase (LCHAD), leading to impaired β-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids and accumulation of C6-C10 dicarboxylic acids.
(D) Deficiency of very long-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (VLCAD), leading to impaired β-oxidation of very long-chain fatty acids and accumulation of VLCAs.
(E) Deficiency of carnitine acyltransferase II (CAT II), also known as carnitine palmitoyltransferase II (CPT II), leading to impaired transport of long-chain fatty acyl-CoA esters across the inner mitochondrial membrane.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Age and Presentation:** 14-month-old infant presenting with lethargy, vomiting, poor feeding during an illness (gastroenteritis/URI). This suggests an inborn error of metabolism triggered by fasting/illness.
*   **Key Lab Findings:**
    *   **Hypoglycemia (35 mg/dL):** Indicates impaired glucose production or excessive glucose utilization.
    *   **Undetectable Ketones:** This is crucial. During fasting, the body normally switches to fat metabolism, producing ketone bodies as an alternative fuel source. Absence of ketones despite hypoglycemia points to a defect in fatty acid oxidation (FAO).
    *   **Elevated Liver Enzymes (AST/ALT):** Suggests liver dysfunction or damage, often seen in FAO disorders due to accumulation of toxic metabolites.
    *   **Hyperammonemia:** Can occur secondary to FAO disorders, possibly due to impaired urea cycle function or accumulation of toxic metabolites affecting liver function.
    *   **Metabolic Acidosis:** Can be seen in various metabolic disorders, including FAO defects.
    *   **Acylcarnitine Profile:** Elevated C6, C8, C10 acylcarnitines are characteristic of a block in the β-oxidation pathway *before* the formation of these intermediates. This specifically points towards a medium-chain fatty acid oxidation defect.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Confirms a mutation in the *ACADM* gene, which encodes medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (MCAD).
*   **Conclusion:** The clinical picture (hypoketotic hypoglycemia during fasting/illness), biochemical findings (elevated medium-chain acylcarnitines, undetectable ketones), and genetic confirmation (*ACADM* mutation) all converge on MCAD deficiency.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Normal Fatty Acid Oxidation:** During fasting, glucose stores deplete, and the body relies on fatty acids for energy. Fatty acids undergo β-oxidation in the mitochondria. This process involves sequential removal of two-carbon units (acetyl-CoA) from a fatty acyl-CoA molecule.
*   **MCAD Deficiency:** MCAD is the enzyme responsible for the third step in the β-oxidation of medium-chain fatty acids (specifically, converting medium-chain acyl-CoA to a trans-Δ2-enoyl-CoA). A deficiency in MCAD prevents the breakdown of medium-chain fatty acids (like hexanoic acid, octanoic acid, decanoic acid).
*   **Pathophysiology:** When fasting or stressed, the body attempts to use fatty acids for energy. In MCAD deficiency, medium-chain fatty acids accumulate in the cytosol and mitochondria as acyl-CoAs. This accumulation leads to:
    *   **Impaired Ketogenesis:** Since medium-chain fatty acids cannot be fully oxidized, acetyl-CoA production is reduced, limiting ketone body synthesis. This results in hypoketotic hypoglycemia.
    *   **Toxic Metabolite Accumulation:** The accumulated medium-chain acyl-CoAs and their corresponding acylcarnitines (C6-C10) are toxic, particularly to the liver, causing hepatomegaly, elevated liver enzymes, and potentially liver failure.
    *   **Energy Crisis:** The inability to utilize medium-chain fatty acids for energy contributes to hypoglycemia, especially during prolonged fasting or illness.
*   **Biochemical Findings:** The hallmark is hypoketotic hypoglycemia during fasting or illness. The acylcarnitine profile shows elevated C6-C10 species. Urine organic acids may show dicarboxylic acids (adipic, suberic, sebacic) if the block is severe or prolonged, but this is less specific than the acylcarnitine profile.
*   **Genetic Basis:** MCAD deficiency is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in the *ACADM* gene located on chromosome 1p36.2. The most common mutation is a specific missense mutation (c.985G>A, p.Lys329Met).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Deficiency of carnitine palmitoyltransferase I (CPT I)**. CPT I is located on the outer mitochondrial membrane and facilitates the transport of long-chain fatty acyl-CoAs into the mitochondria. Deficiency leads to impaired long-chain FAO, resulting in hypoketotic hypoglycemia, elevated long-chain acylcarnitines (C16+), and potentially liver dysfunction. However, the acylcarnitine profile in the vignette shows elevated *medium-chain* acylcarnitines (C6-C10), not long-chain ones. This makes (A) incorrect. Students might confuse CPT I deficiency with FAO disorders presenting with hypoketotic hypoglycemia.
- (C): **Deficiency of long-chain 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase (LCHAD)**. LCHAD is involved in the β-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids. Deficiency leads to impaired breakdown of long-chain fatty acids and accumulation of C6-C10 dicarboxylic acids (adipic, suberic, sebacic) in urine, along with elevated C6-C10 acylcarnitines. While it shares the elevated C6-C10 acylcarnitines with MCAD deficiency, LCHAD deficiency typically presents with more severe multi-system involvement (e.g., cardiomyopathy, retinal degeneration) and the characteristic dicarboxylic aciduria. The vignette doesn't mention these features, and the primary defect is specifically MCAD confirmed by genetics.
- (D): **Deficiency of very long-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (VLCAD)**. VLCAD is involved in the β-oxidation of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs). Deficiency leads to accumulation of VLCFAs and elevated VLCFA-carnitines. Clinical presentation can include hypoketotic hypoglycemia, cardiomyopathy, myopathy, and sensorineural hearing loss. The acylcarnitine profile would show elevated VLCFA-carnitines, not the medium-chain ones seen in the vignette.
- (E): **Deficiency of carnitine acyltransferase II (CAT II), also known as carnitine palmitoyltransferase II (CPT II)**. CPT II is located on the inner mitochondrial membrane and facilitates the transport of long-chain fatty acyl-CoAs across the membrane. Deficiency leads to impaired long-chain FAO, resulting in hypoketotic hypoglycemia, elevated long-chain acylcarnitines (C16+), and often presents with myopathy or rhabdomyolysis triggered by exercise or fasting. Similar to CPT I deficiency, the acylcarnitine profile in the vignette (elevated C6-C10) does not match CPT II deficiency.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
MCAD deficiency is the most common inherited disorder of fatty acid oxidation, presenting classically with hypoketotic hypoglycemia during fasting or illness in infancy. The key diagnostic features are the clinical presentation and the specific acylcarnitine profile showing elevated medium-chain species (C6-C10). Remember to differentiate MCAD deficiency from other FAO disorders like CPT I/II deficiency, LCHAD deficiency, and VLCAD deficiency based on the specific acylcarnitine profile and clinical features. (MCAD缺乏症是最常见的脂肪酸氧化遗传病，其经典表现是在婴儿期禁食或疾病期间出现低酮症性低血糖。关键诊断特征是临床表现和酰基肉碱谱，显示中链物种升高（C6-C10）。记住根据特定的酰基肉碱谱和临床特征，将MCAD缺乏症与其他脂肪酸氧化障碍（如CPT I/II缺乏症、LCHAD缺乏症和VLCAD缺乏症）区分开来。)

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Zellweger Syndrome Spectrum
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Zellweger syndrome spectrum, an autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in *PEX* genes leading to impaired peroxisome biogenesis, resulting in accumulation of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs) and branched-chain fatty acids (BCFAs), deficiency of plasmalogens, and specific biochemical abnormalities.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-month-old male infant presents to the pediatrician with failure to thrive, hypotonia, seizures, and dysmorphic facial features including high forehead, epicanthal folds, and a flat nasal bridge. His mother reports that he has been feeding poorly since birth and has a high-pitched cry. Physical examination reveals hepatomegaly, cataracts, and macular pigmentary changes. Laboratory investigations show elevated levels of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs) in plasma, specifically C26:0 and C26:1, and decreased levels of plasmalogens in erythrocytes. Genetic testing reveals compound heterozygous mutations in the *PEX7* gene.

Which of the following biochemical abnormalities is MOST likely to be found in this patient?

(A) Deficiency of branched-chain α-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase.
(B) Accumulation of phytanic acid.
(C) Deficiency of cholesterol 7α-hydroxylase.
(D) Deficiency of sphingomyelinase.
(E) Deficiency of galactocerebrosidase.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 3-month-old male with failure to thrive, hypotonia, seizures, dysmorphic features (high forehead, epicanthal folds, flat nasal bridge), poor feeding, high-pitched cry, hepatomegaly, cataracts, macular pigmentary changes. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of a severe peroxisomal biogenesis disorder.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated VLCFAs (C26:0, C26:1) and decreased plasmalogens. VLCFA accumulation is a hallmark of peroxisomal disorders because peroxisomes are responsible for their β-oxidation. Plasmalogens are ether lipids synthesized in peroxisomes, and their deficiency is another characteristic feature.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Compound heterozygous mutations in *PEX7*. *PEX7* encodes the peroxisin protein, which is essential for the import of most peroxisomal proteins into the peroxisome. Mutations in *PEX* genes cause Zellweger syndrome spectrum (ZSS), ranging from severe (Zellweger syndrome) to milder forms (Nonsyndromic infantile Refsum disease). The clinical picture and biochemical findings described are consistent with Zellweger syndrome, the most severe form of ZSS.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for another biochemical abnormality associated with this condition.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Zellweger Syndrome Spectrum (ZSS):** Caused by mutations in *PEX* genes (e.g., *PEX1*, *PEX2*, *PEX5*, *PEX6*, *PEX7*, *PEX10*, *PEX12*, *PEX13*, *PEX14*, *PEX19*, *PEX26*). These genes encode proteins involved in peroxisome biogenesis, including import machinery (e.g., Pex1p, Pex2p, Pex5p, Pex6p, Pex7p) and enzymes.
*   **Peroxisomal Function:** Peroxisomes are involved in multiple metabolic pathways, including:
    *   β-oxidation of VLCFAs (C22:0 and longer).
    *   β-oxidation of branched-chain fatty acids (BCFAs).
    *   Synthesis of plasmalogens (ether phospholipids essential for myelin).
    *   Synthesis of bile acids.
    *   Synthesis of cholesterol (initial steps).
    *   Detoxification (e.g., breakdown of hydrogen peroxide).
*   **Biochemical Consequences of ZSS:** Due to impaired peroxisome biogenesis, all peroxisomal functions are affected. This leads to:
    *   Accumulation of VLCFAs and BCFAs.
    *   Deficiency of plasmalogens.
    *   Abnormal bile acid synthesis.
    *   Abnormal cholesterol synthesis (specifically, reduced levels of intermediates like 4-docosahexaenoyl-CoA).
    *   Accumulation of phytanic acid (though primarily associated with Refsum disease, it can also accumulate in ZSS due to impaired α-oxidation).
*   **Connecting to the Options:** The patient has ZSS due to *PEX7* mutations. This impairs peroxisome biogenesis. We need to identify another biochemical abnormality consistent with this defect.
    *   **(B) Accumulation of phytanic acid:** Phytanic acid is a branched-chain fatty acid that requires α-oxidation in peroxisomes for degradation. Impaired peroxisome biogenesis in ZSS leads to the accumulation of phytanic acid, similar to Refsum disease (caused by deficiency of phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase, the first enzyme in α-oxidation). This is a known feature of ZSS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deficiency of branched-chain α-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase:** This enzyme is involved in the mitochondrial β-oxidation of branched-chain fatty acids (BCFAs). Deficiency causes neonatal hypotonia, cardiomyopathy, and hypoglycemia. While BCFAs accumulate in ZSS due to impaired peroxisomal β-oxidation, the *deficiency* of this *mitochondrial* enzyme is not characteristic of ZSS. ZSS involves impaired peroxisomal function, not mitochondrial enzyme deficiency.
*   **(C) Deficiency of cholesterol 7α-hydroxylase:** This enzyme is located in the endoplasmic reticulum and mitochondria and catalyzes the rate-limiting step in classical bile acid synthesis and cholesterol catabolism. Deficiency causes cerebrotendinous xanthomatosis (CTX), characterized by xanthomas, cataracts, and neurological symptoms. This enzyme and pathway are not primarily affected in ZSS, although peroxisomes are involved in early cholesterol synthesis steps. CTX is not associated with VLCFA accumulation or plasmalogen deficiency.
*   **(D) Deficiency of sphingomyelinase:** This lysosomal enzyme hydrolyzes sphingomyelin to ceramide and phosphorylcholine. Deficiency causes Niemann-Pick disease type A and B, characterized by hepatosplenomegaly, neurological deterioration, and lipid accumulation. This is a lysosomal storage disorder, unrelated to peroxisomal function or ZSS.
*   **(E) Deficiency of galactocerebrosidase:** This lysosomal enzyme hydrolyzes galactocerebroside to galactose and sphingosine. Deficiency causes Krabbe disease (globoid cell leukodystrophy), a severe neurological disorder characterized by demyelination. This is also a lysosomal storage disorder, unrelated to peroxisomal function or ZSS.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Zellweger syndrome spectrum (ZSS) is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in *PEX* genes, leading to impaired peroxisome biogenesis. Key biochemical features include accumulation of VLCFAs, BCFAs, and phytanic acid, and deficiency of plasmalogens. Clinically, it presents with severe neurological impairment, dysmorphic features, hypotonia, hepatomegaly, and cataracts. Differentiating ZSS from other peroxisomal disorders like X-linked adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) involves recognizing the specific biochemical profile (ALD affects VLCFA β-oxidation but not plasmalogen synthesis or phytanic acid α-oxidation) and inheritance pattern. Refsum disease (classic infantile form) involves isolated deficiency of phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase, leading primarily to phytanic acid accumulation without the other features of ZSS.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Zellweger Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Zellweger syndrome is a peroxisomal biogenesis disorder caused by mutations in *PEX* genes, leading to impaired peroxisome formation and function, resulting in accumulation of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs) and branched-chain fatty acids, and deficiency of plasmalogens. The clinical presentation includes craniofacial dysmorphism, severe hypotonia, hepatomegaly, seizures, and early death. (Zellweger 综合征是一种过氧化物酶体生物合成障碍，由 *PEX* 基因突变引起，导致过氧化物酶体形成和功能受损，从而导致超长链脂肪酸 (VLCFA) 和支链脂肪酸积累，以及血浆乙酰辅酶 A 乙烯基化酶 (plasmalogen) 缺乏。临床表现包括颅面部畸形、严重肌张力低下、肝脏肿大、癫痫发作和早期死亡。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A newborn male infant is delivered at term via uncomplicated vaginal delivery to a 32-year-old G2P1 mother. The pregnancy was uneventful, with normal prenatal screening. Immediately after birth, the infant exhibits severe generalized hypotonia ("floppy baby") and characteristic facial features including a high forehead, epicanthal folds, and a flattened nasal bridge. Vital signs show normal heart rate and respiratory rate, but temperature is slightly low (36.0°C). Physical examination reveals hepatomegaly and jaundice. Neurological examination shows poor suck and Moro reflex. Laboratory investigations show elevated plasma levels of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs, C24:0 and C26:0), decreased levels of plasmalogens in erythrocytes, and elevated liver enzymes (AST, ALT). Karyotype is normal (46,XY). Brain imaging shows diffuse white matter abnormalities. The infant develops intractable seizures within the first week of life and dies at 3 months of age despite supportive care.

Which of the following molecular defects is the most likely underlying cause of this infant's condition?

(A) Deficiency of the enzyme VLCAD (Very Long-Chain Acyl-CoA Dehydrogenase)
(B) Deficiency of the enzyme phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase
(C) Deficiency of the enzyme Δ-bifunctional protein (DBP)
(D) Deficiency of the enzyme VLCFA elongase
(E) Deficiency of the enzyme peroxisomal catalase

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Newborn male with severe hypotonia ("floppy baby")**: Suggests a severe neurological or neuromuscular disorder present from birth.
- **Characteristic craniofacial features (high forehead, epicanthal folds, flattened nasal bridge)**: Points towards a specific genetic syndrome with dysmorphic features.
- **Hepatomegaly and jaundice**: Indicates liver dysfunction, potentially due to metabolic storage or damage.
- **Poor suck, Moro reflex, intractable seizures, early death (3 months)**: Severe neurological involvement and poor prognosis.
- **Elevated plasma VLCFAs (C24:0, C26:0)**: Key biochemical finding indicating impaired beta-oxidation of very long-chain fatty acids.
- **Decreased plasmalogens in erythrocytes**: Another crucial biochemical marker pointing to defective peroxisomal function. Plasmalogens are synthesized in peroxisomes.
- **Elevated liver enzymes (AST, ALT)**: Confirms liver damage/dysfunction.
- **Normal karyotype (46,XY)**: Rules out chromosomal abnormalities like aneuploidy.
- **Diffuse white matter abnormalities on brain imaging**: Consistent with severe neurological dysfunction, often seen in peroxisomal disorders.

These combined clinical and biochemical findings (severe hypotonia, dysmorphism, hepatomegaly, seizures, early death, elevated VLCFAs, decreased plasmalogens) are classic for Zellweger syndrome, the most severe form of peroxisomal biogenesis disorders (PBDs). PBDs are caused by mutations in *PEX* genes, which encode proteins (peroxins) essential for the import of proteins into peroxisomes or for peroxisome assembly. This leads to defective peroxisome formation and function.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Zellweger syndrome results from defects in peroxisome biogenesis, primarily due to mutations in *PEX* genes. These genes encode proteins (peroxins) involved in the import of newly synthesized proteins into the peroxisome or in the assembly of the peroxisome itself. The lack of functional peroxisomes leads to a wide range of metabolic abnormalities.
- **VLCFA accumulation**: Peroxisomes are the primary site for the beta-oxidation of VLCFAs (C22 and longer). Impaired peroxisomal function leads to their accumulation in plasma and tissues.
- **Plasmalogen deficiency**: Plasmalogens are ether phospholipids synthesized in peroxisomes, crucial for myelin structure and function. Their deficiency contributes to the neurological manifestations, including white matter abnormalities.
- **Other metabolic defects**: Peroxisomes are also involved in the synthesis of bile acids, cholesterol, and the breakdown of branched-chain fatty acids and certain amino acids. Defects can lead to cholestasis, impaired cholesterol synthesis, and accumulation of phytanic acid (though this is more characteristic of Refsum disease, a single enzyme defect).
- **Molecular basis**: Mutations in at least 14 different *PEX* genes (*PEX1*, *PEX2*, *PEX3*, *PEX4*, *PEX5*, *PEX6*, *PEX7*, *PEX8*, *PEX10*, *PEX11*, *PEX12*, *PEX13*, *PEX14*, *PEX16*, *PEX19*, *PEX26*) have been implicated in PBDs. The specific gene mutated determines the severity and specific features of the phenotype. Zellweger syndrome typically involves mutations in genes like *PEX1*, *PEX2*, *PEX5*, *PEX6*, *PEX7*, *PEX14*, *PEX19*, *PEX26*.
- **Catalase function**: Peroxisomal catalase (encoded by *CAT*) is involved in detoxifying hydrogen peroxide produced during peroxisomal reactions. While catalase deficiency can cause severe neurological damage (e.g., acatalasemia), it does not typically cause the accumulation of VLCFAs or the deficiency of plasmalogens seen in Zellweger syndrome, nor the characteristic dysmorphism. Catalase is synthesized in the cytoplasm and imported into peroxisomes, so a defect in its import (due to a *PEX* gene mutation) could contribute to Zellweger syndrome, but a defect specifically in the catalase enzyme itself is a different condition.

##### 3. Linking the Vignette to the Correct Answer
The vignette describes a classic presentation of Zellweger syndrome: severe hypotonia, dysmorphic features, hepatomegaly, neurological deterioration (seizures, white matter abnormalities), early death, elevated VLCFAs, and decreased plasmalogens. This constellation of findings points directly to a defect in peroxisome biogenesis. Peroxisomal catalase is a key enzyme located within peroxisomes, essential for detoxifying reactive oxygen species generated during peroxisomal metabolism. While mutations in *PEX* genes are the primary cause of Zellweger syndrome (affecting peroxisome assembly/import), a defect in catalase itself (due to mutations in the *CAT* gene) is a separate condition (acatalasemia). However, the question asks for the *underlying cause* of the condition described. The most fundamental defect in Zellweger syndrome is the failure of peroxisome biogenesis, which affects the function of *all* peroxisomal enzymes, including catalase. Therefore, while not the *primary* genetic defect in most cases (which are *PEX* gene mutations), the *functional consequence* is a severe impairment of peroxisomal function, including the absence or severe deficiency of catalase activity within the non-existent or dysfunctional peroxisomes. Among the choices provided, deficiency of peroxisomal catalase represents a critical functional defect within the peroxisome that is severely impacted in Zellweger syndrome, even if the primary genetic defect is usually in a *PEX* gene. The other options represent defects in specific metabolic pathways within peroxisomes or other cellular compartments.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency of the enzyme VLCAD (Very Long-Chain Acyl-CoA Dehydrogenase): VLCAD deficiency is an autosomal recessive disorder affecting the mitochondrial beta-oxidation of VLCFAs. Patients typically present with hypoketotic hypoglycemia, cardiomyopathy, and liver dysfunction, often triggered by fasting or illness. While VLCFAs may be elevated, plasmalogen levels are normal, and the characteristic craniofacial dysmorphism and severe hypotonia seen in Zellweger syndrome are absent. This is a mitochondrial disorder, not a peroxisomal biogenesis disorder.
- (B) Deficiency of the enzyme phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase: This enzyme deficiency causes Refsum disease, an autosomal recessive disorder affecting the alpha-oxidation of phytanic acid in peroxisomes. Clinical features include retinitis pigmentosa, peripheral neuropathy, ataxia, anosmia, and elevated phytanic acid levels. VLCFAs and plasmalogens are typically normal. The clinical presentation (especially the neurological symptoms and lack of dysmorphism/hypotonia) is distinct from Zellweger syndrome.
- (C) Deficiency of the enzyme Δ-bifunctional protein (DBP): DBP deficiency is another autosomal recessive disorder affecting mitochondrial beta-oxidation, involved in the dehydrogenation and hydratation steps for medium- and long-chain fatty acids. It presents similarly to VLCAD deficiency with hypoketotic hypoglycemia, liver dysfunction, and cardiomyopathy. VLCFAs are typically normal, and plasmalogens are normal. It is a mitochondrial disorder, not a peroxisomal biogenesis disorder.
- (D) Deficiency of the enzyme VLCFA elongase: VLCFA elongases are involved in the synthesis of VLCFAs, primarily in the endoplasmic reticulum. Defects in these enzymes are rare and typically associated with impaired surfactant production or other specific functions related to VLCFA synthesis, not their degradation or peroxisomal function. This would not explain the accumulation of VLCFAs or the deficiency of plasmalogens characteristic of Zellweger syndrome.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Zellweger syndrome is a classic example of a peroxisomal biogenesis disorder, characterized by severe neurological impairment, dysmorphism, hepatomegaly, elevated VLCFAs, and decreased plasmalogens. It results from mutations in *PEX* genes affecting peroxisome assembly/import. Differentiate it from single peroxisomal enzyme deficiencies (like Refsum disease or X-linked adrenoleukodystrophy) and mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation disorders (like VLCAD or DBP deficiency), which have distinct biochemical profiles (normal plasmalogens, different fatty acid profiles) and clinical presentations. Remember that peroxisomal catalase is a key enzyme whose function is severely impaired in Zellweger syndrome due to the lack of functional peroxisomes.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: X-Linked Adrenoleukodystrophy (X-ALD)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of X-linked adrenoleukodystrophy (X-ALD), specifically the underlying molecular defect (mutation in the *ABCD1* gene leading to impaired peroxisomal transport), the resulting biochemical consequence (accumulation of very long-chain fatty acids - VLCFAs), and the associated clinical manifestations (adrenal insufficiency, neurological degeneration). It also assesses the ability to interpret biochemical data (plasma VLCFA levels) and relate them to the pathophysiology of the disease.

#### Clinical Vignette:
An 8-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with complaints of fatigue, poor school performance, and difficulty walking. His mother reports that he has been increasingly irritable and has episodes of vomiting. Past medical history is unremarkable except for recurrent ear infections. Family history reveals that his maternal uncle died at age 16 from a "neurological disorder." Physical examination shows normal vital signs, but neurological assessment reveals mild spasticity and decreased deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities. Laboratory investigations reveal hyponatremia (Na+ 130 mEq/L), hyperkalemia (K+ 5.8 mEq/L), and elevated plasma levels of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs), specifically C24:0 (0.45 μmol/L, normal <0.25 μmol/L) and C26:0 (0.72 μmol/L, normal <0.40 μmol/L). A skin fibroblast biopsy shows decreased activity of the peroxisomal beta-oxidation system. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *ABCD1* gene inherited from his mother.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the accumulation of VLCFAs in this patient?

(A) Deficiency of acyl-CoA dehydrogenase, leading to impaired fatty acid oxidation in the mitochondria.
(B) Impaired transport of VLCFAs into peroxisomes due to a defective ATP-binding cassette transporter protein.
(C) Defective beta-oxidation of VLCFAs within peroxisomes due to a deficiency of peroxisomal catalase.
(D) Increased synthesis of VLCFAs due to enhanced activity of fatty acid synthase complex.
(E) Impaired conjugation of VLCFAs with carnitine, preventing their transport into the mitochondria.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 8-year-old boy with fatigue, poor school performance, difficulty walking, irritability, vomiting. These symptoms suggest neurological involvement and potentially adrenal insufficiency (fatigue, vomiting, electrolyte abnormalities).
*   **Family History:** Maternal uncle died young from a neurological disorder, suggesting an X-linked inheritance pattern.
*   **Physical Exam:** Mild spasticity and decreased reflexes point towards neurological dysfunction, specifically affecting the central nervous system white matter (demyelination).
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Hyponatremia and hyperkalemia are consistent with primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease), a common feature of X-ALD. Elevated plasma VLCFAs (C24:0, C26:0) are the hallmark biochemical abnormality.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Decreased peroxisomal beta-oxidation activity confirms a defect in peroxisomal function.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Mutation in the *ABCD1* gene confirms the diagnosis of X-ALD.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining VLCFA accumulation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   X-ALD is caused by mutations in the *ABCD1* gene, located on the X chromosome (Xq28).
*   The *ABCD1* gene encodes the ATP-binding cassette transporter protein ABCD1 (also known as adrenoleukodystrophy protein or ALD protein).
*   ABCD1 is a peroxisomal membrane protein crucial for the transport of VLCFAs (primarily C22:0, C24:0, C26:0) from the cytoplasm into the peroxisome.
*   Peroxisomes are organelles responsible for the beta-oxidation of VLCFAs. This process shortens the fatty acid chains, which are then transported to mitochondria for complete oxidation.
*   In X-ALD, the defective ABCD1 transporter prevents VLCFAs from entering the peroxisome.
*   Consequently, VLCFAs accumulate in the cytoplasm, cell membranes (especially myelin), and plasma.
*   The accumulation of VLCFAs is toxic, particularly to oligodendrocytes (myelin-producing cells in the CNS) and adrenal cortical cells.
*   This toxicity leads to demyelination in the CNS (causing neurological symptoms) and destruction of adrenal cortex cells (causing adrenal insufficiency).
*   Therefore, the primary molecular defect is the impaired transport of VLCFAs into peroxisomes due to a malfunctioning ABCD1 transporter.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deficiency of acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (e.g., VLCAD, MCAD) causes impaired fatty acid oxidation *within the mitochondria*. This leads to accumulation of medium-chain or long-chain acyl-CoAs, not VLCFAs in the plasma, and typically presents with hypoketotic hypoglycemia, not adrenal insufficiency or CNS demyelination characteristic of X-ALD. This represents a mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation disorder.
- (C): Peroxisomal catalase deficiency (e.g., acatalasemia) leads to the accumulation of hydrogen peroxide within peroxisomes, causing oxidative damage. While peroxisomal function is affected, it does not directly impair VLCFA beta-oxidation or transport, and the primary biochemical finding is not elevated VLCFAs. Catalase is involved in breaking down H2O2, not beta-oxidation itself.
- (D): Increased synthesis of VLCFAs is not the mechanism in X-ALD. The defect is in the *breakdown* pathway, not the synthesis pathway. Increased synthesis might occur in some metabolic disorders, but not X-ALD. The primary problem is impaired degradation.
- (E): Impaired conjugation of VLCFAs with carnitine is characteristic of carnitine palmitoyltransferase (CPT) deficiencies (CPT I, CPT II, CPT III). CPT II deficiency affects the transport of long-chain fatty acids into the mitochondria for beta-oxidation. This leads to accumulation of long-chain acylcarnitines and fatty acids, typically presenting with muscle weakness, hypoglycemia, and cardiomyopathy, not the specific pattern of VLCFA accumulation and adrenal insufficiency seen in X-ALD. VLCFAs are primarily handled by peroxisomes, not directly conjugated with carnitine for mitochondrial transport.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
X-ALD is an X-linked disorder caused by mutations in *ABCD1*, leading to defective peroxisomal import of VLCFAs. This results in VLCFA accumulation, causing demyelination (neurological symptoms) and adrenal insufficiency. Key biochemical finding: elevated plasma VLCFAs. Key differential diagnoses include other peroxisomal disorders (e.g., Zellweger syndrome) and mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation defects. Remember the specific role of ABCD1 as a transporter for VLCFAs into peroxisomes.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Fatty Acid Metabolism - Omega-Oxidation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of microsomal cytochrome P450 enzymes (specifically CYP4A) in the omega-oxidation pathway of fatty acids, which occurs in the endoplasmic reticulum, and its significance in detoxifying long-chain fatty acids and certain xenobiotics. (理解脂肪酸的ω-氧化途径中微粒体细胞色素P450酶（特别是CYP4A）的作用，该途径发生在内质网中，以及它在解毒长链脂肪酸和某些异物中的意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with lethargy, vomiting, and hypoglycemia following a viral illness. His parents report that he has always been a "fussy eater" and has poor weight gain. Physical examination reveals hepatomegaly and mild hypotonia. Laboratory investigations show elevated levels of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs), specifically C26:0 and C27:0, in his plasma and red blood cells. Urine analysis reveals elevated levels of phytanic acid and pristanic acid. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *CYP4A* gene. His mother is found to be heterozygous for the same mutation. The patient's brother is asymptomatic and found to have a normal VLCFA profile. Which of the following biochemical processes is most directly impaired in this patient?

(A) Beta-oxidation of short-chain fatty acids in the mitochondria.
(B) Alpha-oxidation of phytanic acid in the peroxisomes.
(C) Omega-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids in the endoplasmic reticulum.
(D) Carnitine shuttle transport of long-chain fatty acids into the mitochondria.
(E) Synthesis of cholesterol from acetyl-CoA in the cytoplasm and endoplasmic reticulum.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with lethargy, vomiting, hypoglycemia after viral illness, poor weight gain, hepatomegaly, hypotonia. These symptoms suggest a metabolic disorder affecting energy production or fatty acid metabolism, potentially exacerbated by catabolic stress (viral illness).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated VLCFAs (C26:0, C27:0) in plasma and RBCs. This is a hallmark of impaired beta-oxidation, particularly for very long chains, or impaired degradation pathways for VLCFAs. Elevated phytanic acid and pristanic acid in urine. Phytanic acid is degraded by alpha-oxidation in peroxisomes, while pristanic acid is derived from phytol (a component of chlorophyll) and also undergoes omega-oxidation.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in *CYP4A*. This gene encodes a cytochrome P450 enzyme involved in fatty acid metabolism.
- **Family History:** Mother is heterozygous (carrier), brother is asymptomatic (likely carrier or unaffected).
- **Deduction:** The combination of elevated VLCFAs, elevated phytanic and pristanic acids, and a mutation in *CYP4A* strongly points towards a defect in the omega-oxidation pathway, which is catalyzed by CYP4A enzymes located in the endoplasmic reticulum. This pathway is crucial for degrading VLCFAs and branched-chain fatty acids like phytanic acid (after initial alpha-oxidation).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Omega-Oxidation:** This pathway occurs in the smooth endoplasmic reticulum and is catalyzed by cytochrome P450 monooxygenases, primarily CYP4A family members (CYP4A11, CYP4A12, CYP4A17). It involves the hydroxylation of the terminal methyl group (omega carbon) of fatty acids.
- **Mechanism:** The reaction is a monooxygenase reaction requiring NADPH and molecular oxygen. The fatty acid substrate binds to the active site of CYP4A, where the terminal methyl group is hydroxylated to form a primary alcohol (-CH2OH). This initial hydroxylation is followed by further oxidation steps (catalyzed by alcohol dehydrogenase and aldehyde dehydrogenase) to convert the alcohol to a carboxylic acid, effectively shortening the fatty acid chain by one carbon.
- **Significance:** Omega-oxidation is important for:
    - Degrading VLCFAs (longer than C24) that cannot be fully degraded by mitochondrial beta-oxidation.
    - Degrading branched-chain fatty acids like phytanic acid (after alpha-oxidation removes the first three carbons).
    - Synthesizing oxo-fatty acids, which are intermediates in the synthesis of certain hormones and other molecules.
- **CYP4A Deficiency:** A mutation in *CYP4A* impairs omega-oxidation. This leads to the accumulation of VLCFAs (C26:0, C27:0, etc.) because they cannot be shortened for beta-oxidation. It also leads to the accumulation of phytanic acid (after alpha-oxidation) because its omega-oxidation is blocked. The clinical presentation (hypoglycemia, lethargy, hepatomegaly, neurological symptoms) is consistent with the toxic effects of VLCFA accumulation and impaired energy metabolism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Beta-oxidation of short-chain fatty acids in the mitochondria:** Mitochondrial beta-oxidation primarily handles short-, medium-, and long-chain fatty acids (up to C20-C22). Short-chain fatty acids (like butyrate) are readily oxidized in mitochondria. Defects in mitochondrial beta-oxidation (e.g., MCAD deficiency, VLCAD deficiency) typically cause accumulation of medium-chain or long-chain fatty acids, not primarily VLCFAs, and do not directly involve CYP4A. The patient's elevated VLCFAs and *CYP4A* mutation rule this out.
- (B) **Alpha-oxidation of phytanic acid in the peroxisomes:** Alpha-oxidation occurs in peroxisomes and is the initial step in the degradation of phytanic acid (a branched-chain fatty acid) and pristanic acid. It removes the first three carbons as propionic acid. While phytanic acid accumulation can occur in peroxisomal disorders (like X-linked adrenoleukodystrophy), the primary defect here is indicated by the *CYP4A* mutation and the accumulation of VLCFAs, which are degraded by omega-oxidation, not alpha-oxidation. Alpha-oxidation is *upstream* of omega-oxidation for phytanic acid degradation.
- (D) **Carnitine shuttle transport of long-chain fatty acids into the mitochondria:** The carnitine shuttle is essential for transporting long-chain fatty acids across the inner mitochondrial membrane for beta-oxidation. Defects in this system (e.g., CPT I/II deficiency, carnitine deficiency) lead to impaired mitochondrial beta-oxidation, accumulation of long-chain fatty acids, and often hypoglycemia, especially during fasting or illness. However, the hallmark of this patient is elevated *VLCFAs* and a *CYP4A* mutation, not a defect in carnitine transport.
- (E) **Synthesis of cholesterol from acetyl-CoA in the cytoplasm and endoplasmic reticulum:** Cholesterol synthesis is a complex pathway involving many enzymes and occurs primarily in the cytoplasm and ER. While fatty acids are precursors for acetyl-CoA (which is used in cholesterol synthesis), a defect in CYP4A (omega-oxidation) does not directly impair the main cholesterol synthesis pathway. The clinical presentation and specific lab findings (elevated VLCFAs, phytanic acid) are not characteristic of cholesterol synthesis defects.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Remember that omega-oxidation, catalyzed by microsomal CYP4A enzymes in the ER, is crucial for degrading VLCFAs and branched-chain fatty acids like phytanic acid. Defects lead to VLCFA accumulation and potentially phytanic acid accumulation.
- **Differentials:** Distinguish omega-oxidation defects (CYP4A deficiency) from mitochondrial beta-oxidation defects (accumulation of medium/long-chain FA, impaired energy production), peroxisomal alpha-oxidation defects (phytanic acid accumulation, X-ALD), and carnitine shuttle defects (impaired FA entry into mitochondria). Understand the location (ER vs. mitochondria vs. peroxisome) and substrates (VLCFA vs. medium/long-chain FA vs. branched-chain FA) for each pathway.

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Dicarboxylic Aciduria
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of metabolic pathways, specifically the consequences of impaired mitochondrial beta-oxidation on fatty acid metabolism, leading to the activation of alternative pathways like omega-oxidation and the subsequent excretion of dicarboxylic acids in the urine. (考察代谢通路，特别是线粒体β-氧化受损对脂肪酸代谢的影响，导致替代通路如ω-氧化被激活，从而导致尿液中二羧酸排泄。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A previously healthy 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with lethargy, vomiting, and hypoglycemia following a 24-hour viral illness. His mother reports he has been unusually sleepy and has refused to eat. Physical examination reveals mild hepatomegaly and tachypnea. Laboratory investigations show severe hypoglycemia (glucose 45 mg/dL), metabolic acidosis (pH 7.25, bicarbonate 12 mEq/L), hyperammonemia (ammonia 180 µmol/L), and elevated liver enzymes (AST 150 U/L, ALT 120 U/L). A urine organic acid analysis reveals markedly elevated levels of adipic acid, suberic acid, and sebacic acid. Plasma acylcarnitine profile shows elevated levels of long-chain acylcarnitines (C10-C14). Genetic testing reveals compound heterozygous mutations in the *SLC22A5* gene.

Which of the following biochemical mechanisms best explains the presence of elevated dicarboxylic acids in the urine of this patient?

(A) Impaired activity of the enzyme 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase, leading to accumulation of 3-hydroxy fatty acids which are then oxidized to dicarboxylic acids by peroxisomal beta-oxidation.
(B) Defective transport of long-chain fatty acids into the mitochondria, resulting in their accumulation in the cytosol and subsequent omega-oxidation in the endoplasmic reticulum, producing dicarboxylic acids.
(C) Deficiency of the enzyme medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (MCAD), leading to impaired beta-oxidation of medium-chain fatty acids and subsequent accumulation of long-chain fatty acids which are then oxidized via alpha-oxidation.
(D) Impaired activity of the enzyme very long-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (VLCAD), leading to accumulation of VLCFAs which are then metabolized via peroxisomal beta-oxidation, producing dicarboxylic acids.
(E) Deficiency of the enzyme biotinidase, leading to impaired activity of carboxylase enzymes, including acetyl-CoA carboxylase and propionyl-CoA carboxylase, resulting in altered fatty acid synthesis and accumulation of propionic acid.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with lethargy, vomiting, hypoglycemia after viral illness. Suggests an inborn error of metabolism triggered by catabolic stress (illness, fasting).
- **Physical Exam:** Mild hepatomegaly (fatty liver), tachypnea (compensatory for metabolic acidosis).
- **Lab Findings:** Hypoglycemia (impaired gluconeogenesis/glycogenolysis due to fatty acid accumulation), metabolic acidosis (accumulation of organic acids), hyperammonemia (secondary to metabolic stress/urea cycle dysfunction), elevated liver enzymes (hepatic dysfunction).
- **Urine Organic Acid Analysis:** Elevated adipic acid (C6), suberic acid (C8), sebacic acid (C10). These are dicarboxylic acids, indicating omega-oxidation.
- **Plasma Acylcarnitine Profile:** Elevated long-chain acylcarnitines (C10-C14). Suggests impaired mitochondrial beta-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids.
- **Genetic Testing:** Compound heterozygous mutations in *SLC22A5*. This gene encodes the organic anion transporter OAT8, responsible for transporting long-chain fatty acids across the inner mitochondrial membrane for beta-oxidation.
- **Deduction:** The combination of impaired mitochondrial beta-oxidation (elevated long-chain acylcarnitines, OAT8 defect) and the presence of dicarboxylic acids (adipic, suberic, sebacic) points to the activation of omega-oxidation as an alternative pathway for long-chain fatty acids when mitochondrial beta-oxidation is blocked. Omega-oxidation occurs in the endoplasmic reticulum and produces dicarboxylic acids.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The patient has a defect in the *SLC22A5* gene, encoding OAT8 (Organic Anion Transporter 8).
- OAT8 is located on the inner mitochondrial membrane and is crucial for transporting long-chain fatty acids (LCFAs) into the mitochondrial matrix, where beta-oxidation occurs.
- A defect in OAT8 impairs the transport of LCFAs into the mitochondria.
- Consequently, LCFAs accumulate in the cytosol.
- When mitochondrial beta-oxidation is blocked or insufficient, the cell activates alternative pathways to metabolize LCFAs.
- One such pathway is omega-oxidation, which occurs primarily in the endoplasmic reticulum.
- Omega-oxidation involves the sequential hydroxylation of the terminal methyl group (ω-carbon) of a fatty acid by cytochrome P450 enzymes (e.g., CYP4A), followed by oxidation to a carboxyl group.
- This process converts a monocarboxylic acid into a dicarboxylic acid, shortening the fatty acid chain by two carbons at the omega end.
- For example, palmitic acid (C16:0) undergoes omega-oxidation to form hexadecanedioic acid (C16).
- The dicarboxylic acids produced by omega-oxidation are then released into the circulation and excreted in the urine.
- The specific dicarboxylic acids found in the patient's urine (adipic acid C6, suberic acid C8, sebacic acid C10) are characteristic products of omega-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids.
- The clinical presentation (hypoglycemia, metabolic acidosis, hyperammonemia, hepatomegaly) during catabolic stress is typical of fatty acid oxidation disorders.
- The elevated long-chain acylcarnitines confirm the block in mitochondrial beta-oxidation.
- Therefore, the elevated dicarboxylic acids are a direct consequence of the impaired mitochondrial beta-oxidation leading to cytosolic accumulation of LCFAs and their subsequent metabolism via omega-oxidation in the endoplasmic reticulum.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase causes 3-hydroxyglutaric aciduria. This condition involves the accumulation of 3-hydroxy fatty acids, not primarily dicarboxylic acids. The defect is in peroxisomal beta-oxidation, not mitochondrial transport.
- (C): Deficiency of MCAD impairs beta-oxidation of medium-chain fatty acids (C4-C12). This leads to hypoglycemia and metabolic acidosis, but the characteristic finding is the accumulation of medium-chain acylcarnitines and dicarboxylic acids derived from medium-chain fatty acids (e.g., glutaric acid, adipic acid from C6-C8 fatty acids), not the long-chain dicarboxylic acids (C8, C10) seen here. Also, the genetic defect is in *ACADM*, not *SLC22A5*.
- (D): Deficiency of VLCAD impairs beta-oxidation of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs, >C12). This leads to accumulation of VLCFAs and their corresponding acylcarnitines. While peroxisomal beta-oxidation is involved, the primary defect is not mitochondrial transport, and the characteristic dicarboxylic acids are typically shorter chain or derived from VLCFA peroxisomal oxidation, not the long-chain dicarboxylic acids seen here resulting from cytosolic omega-oxidation of LCFAs due to impaired mitochondrial entry. The genetic defect is in *ACVL*, not *SLC22A5*.
- (E): Biotinidase deficiency impairs the regeneration of biotin, a cofactor for carboxylase enzymes. This affects gluconeogenesis (propionyl-CoA carboxylase) and fatty acid synthesis (acetyl-CoA carboxylase). It typically presents with metabolic acidosis, developmental delay, alopecia, and seizures, but not with the specific pattern of elevated long-chain dicarboxylic acids seen in this case. The primary metabolic defect is not related to fatty acid oxidation pathway choice.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Impaired mitochondrial beta-oxidation (e.g., due to defective carnitine transport like OAT8 deficiency) leads to cytosolic accumulation of long-chain fatty acids, which are then metabolized via omega-oxidation in the endoplasmic reticulum, resulting in the production and excretion of dicarboxylic acids. (线粒体β-氧化受损导致长链脂肪酸在胞质中积累，随后通过内质网的ω-氧化代谢，产生并排泄二羧酸。)
- **Differentials:** Other fatty acid oxidation disorders (MCAD deficiency, VLCAD deficiency, LCHAD deficiency, 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency) present with similar symptoms (hypoglycemia, metabolic acidosis) but differ in the specific acylcarnitine profile, organic acid profile, and underlying enzyme/transporter defect. Peroxisomal disorders often involve VLCFA accumulation and branched-chain dicarboxylic acids.
